Then I’m off following that for hours, and the thing I was supposed to be doing kind of falls apart.\n\nDoctor: When you get pulled into these details or ideas, do you find it hard to stop thinking about them or disengage, even if you know you should focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once I latch onto something, it’s really hard to let it go. I can know I’m supposed to be paying attention to the grant call or the experiment, but if my mind catches on a pump noise or some weird data peak, I just keep following it. Hours can go by, and the thing I was actually responsible for ends up getting neglected.\n\nDoctor: When you're deeply engaged in following one of these ideas or details, do you become so absorbed in it that it's hard to stop, even if someone tries to interrupt or if it's time to move on to something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly it. I get so absorbed that it’s hard to stop, even if someone needs me to move on. I’ll notice something like a strange pump vibration or an odd peak in old data, and then I’m chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the actual experiment, or the meeting, or whatever people were counting on me for, just gets away from me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m a physical scientist, and lately during a grant call or even a safety meeting, I’ll notice something small—like a pump vibrating differently, or a student making some side comment, or an odd peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m gone. I’ll chase that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing everyone needed from me just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these moments when you get distracted—do you find it hard to focus even when you know the task is important, or do you feel pulled toward these new thoughts almost automatically?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I get pulled automatically. I can know the meeting is important, or that the experiment needs my attention, but if I notice something—like a weird pump vibration or some odd detail in the data—my mind just grabs onto it. Then I’m off following that for hours, and the thing I was supposed to be doing kind of falls apart.\n\nDoctor: When you get pulled into these details or ideas, do you find it hard to stop thinking about them or disengage, even if you know you should focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once I latch onto something, it’s really hard to let it go. I can know I’m supposed to be paying attention to the grant call or the experiment, but if my mind catches on a pump noise or some weird data peak, I just keep following it. Hours can go by, and the thing I was actually responsible for ends up getting neglected.\n\nDoctor: When you're deeply engaged in following one of these ideas or details, do you become so absorbed in it that it's hard to stop, even if someone tries to interrupt or if it's time to move on to something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly it. I get so absorbed that it’s hard to stop, even if someone needs me to move on. I’ll notice something like a strange pump vibration or an odd peak in old data, and then I’m chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the actual experiment, or the meeting, or whatever people were counting on me for, just gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes where you get deeply absorbed in small details or ideas happen only at work, or do they also occur in other settings like at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I notice it most at work because that’s where there are so many details to get pulled into. I haven’t paid as much attention to whether it happens the same way at home or with friends.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times outside of work—like when you’re at home or with friends—where you’ve gotten just as absorbed in a detail or idea, and lost track of what you were supposed to be doing?\n\nPatient: I can think of it mostly at work, honestly. That’s where it’s been really obvious. Like I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting and some small thing catches me—a pump vibration, a student’s side comment, or an odd peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m just gone, chasing it for hours.\n\nI’m not sure I’ve noticed it as clearly at home or with friends, but the same kind of thing happens where I lose the thread of what I was supposed to be doing. The work examples are just the ones that have really fallen apart because people are depending on me.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any specific instances outside of work—like when you’re at home or with friends—where you’ve become so absorbed in a detail or idea that you lost track of time or what you were supposed to be doing?\n\nPatient: I can’t think of a really clear example outside work. The problem I’m coming in for is mostly that I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Like in a grant call or safety meeting, I’ll hear a pump vibration or a student says something offhand, or I’ll notice some weird peak in an old spectrum, and then I’m gone—hours chasing that one thing while the thing I was supposed to be doing falls apart.\n\nAt home or with friends, I’m not sure it shows up in the same obvious way. The work situations are the ones where it’s been really disruptive.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes. Mostly at work, with people there. It’s gotten to where my department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself.
